      <link>https://www.patreon.com/posts/sputnik-151796803</link>
      <itunes:image href="https://c10.patreonusercontent.com/4/patreon-media/p/post/151796803/c0475d202400479ca34122998d14daab/eyJkIjo3MiwiaCI6MzAwMCwic3RyaXBfYWxwaGEiOjEsInciOjMwMDAsIndlYnAiOjB9/1.jpg?token-hash=UXYVkfw7sA05qtjdQWqAHj4gtD3IENl4wMImQEYZDFg%3D"/>
      <itunes:episode>464</itunes:episode>
      <itunes:season>1</itunes:season>
      <itunes:duration>2073</itunes:duration>
      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Sputnik 1&lt;/strong&gt; (&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English" target="_blank"&gt;/ˈspʌtnɪk, ˈspʊtnɪk/&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language" target="_blank"&gt;Russian&lt;/a&gt;: Спутник-1, &lt;em&gt;Satellite 1&lt;/em&gt;), often referred to as simply &lt;strong&gt;Sputnik&lt;/strong&gt;, was the first artificial Earth &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite" target="_blank"&gt;satellite&lt;/a&gt;. It was launched into an elliptical &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_Earth_orbit" target="_blank"&gt;low Earth orbit&lt;/a&gt; by the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union" target="_blank"&gt;Soviet Union&lt;/a&gt; on 4 October 1957 as part of the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_space_program" target="_blank"&gt;Soviet space program&lt;/a&gt;. It sent a radio signal back to Earth for three weeks before its three &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silver-zinc_battery" target="_blank"&gt;silver-zinc batteries&lt;/a&gt; became depleted. Aerodynamic drag caused it to fall back into the atmosphere on 4 January 1958.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <title>Freedom House Ambulance Service &amp; Bessie Coleman</title>
      <link>https://www.patreon.com/posts/freedom-house-150779649</link>
      <itunes:image href="https://c10.patreonusercontent.com/4/patreon-media/p/post/150779649/9aed8ea872b8410f9e7483b173d49e02/eyJkIjo3MiwiaCI6MzAwMCwic3RyaXBfYWxwaGEiOjEsInciOjMwMDAsIndlYnAiOjB9/1.jpg?token-hash=6rnrFY5vD5MCAK9jlaJm00KqSi5ArWdu2w4M9Xa3GsU%3D"/>
      <itunes:episode>462</itunes:episode>
      <itunes:season>1</itunes:season>
      <itunes:duration>1987</itunes:duration>
      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Freedom House Ambulance Service&lt;/strong&gt; was the first &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_medical_services" target="_blank"&gt;emergency medical service&lt;/a&gt; in the United States to be staffed by &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paramedic" target="_blank"&gt;paramedics&lt;/a&gt; with medical training beyond basic first aid.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House_Ambulance_Service#cite_note-JEMS-1" target="_blank"&gt;[1]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House_Ambulance_Service#cite_note-99PI-2" target="_blank"&gt;[2]&lt;/a&gt; Founded in 1967 to serve the predominantly Black &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hill_District_(Pittsburgh)" target="_blank"&gt;Hill District&lt;/a&gt; of &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pittsburgh,_Pennsylvania" target="_blank"&gt;Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania&lt;/a&gt;, it was staffed entirely by African Americans.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House_Ambulance_Service#cite_note-Empowerment-3" target="_blank"&gt;[3]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House_Ambulance_Service#cite_note-Code-4" target="_blank"&gt;[4]&lt;/a&gt; Freedom House Ambulance Service broke medical ground by training its personnel to previously unheard-of standards of emergency medical care for patients en route to hospitals.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House_Ambulance_Service#cite_note-Empowerment-3" target="_blank"&gt;[3]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House_Ambulance_Service#cite_note-Atavist-5" target="_blank"&gt;[5]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House_Ambulance_Service#cite_note-Forgotten-6" target="_blank"&gt;[6]&lt;/a&gt; The paramedic training and ambulance design standards pioneered in the Freedom House Ambulance Service would set the standard for emergency care nationally and even internationally.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House_Ambulance_Service#cite_note-99PI-2" target="_blank"&gt;[2]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House_Ambulance_Service#cite_note-Atavist-5" target="_blank"&gt;[5]&lt;/a&gt; Despite its successes, the ambulance service was closed eight years after it began operating.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House_Ambulance_Service#cite_note-Atavist-5" target="_blank"&gt;[5]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Elizabeth Coleman&lt;/strong&gt; (January 26, 1892 – April 30, 1926)&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessie_Coleman#cite_note-:3-2" target="_blank"&gt;[2]&lt;/a&gt; was an early American &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_aviation" target="_blank"&gt;civil aviator&lt;/a&gt;. She was the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_African-American" target="_blank"&gt;first African-American&lt;/a&gt; woman and first &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States" target="_blank"&gt;Native American&lt;/a&gt; to hold a &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pilot_certification_in_the_United_States" target="_blank"&gt;pilot&lt;/a&gt; license,&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessie_Coleman#cite_note-ABC-3" target="_blank"&gt;[3]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessie_Coleman#cite_note-PBS-4" target="_blank"&gt;[4]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessie_Coleman#cite_note-Women_in_Aviation_International-5" target="_blank"&gt;[5]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessie_Coleman#cite_note-essence-6" target="_blank"&gt;[6]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessie_Coleman#cite_note-af-7" target="_blank"&gt;[7]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessie_Coleman#cite_note-pueblo-8" target="_blank"&gt;[8]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessie_Coleman#cite_note-whm-9" target="_blank"&gt;[9]&lt;/a&gt; and is the earliest known Black person to earn an &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pilot_licensing_and_certification#History" target="_blank"&gt;international pilot's license&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessie_Coleman#cite_note-:0-10" target="_blank"&gt;[10]&lt;/a&gt; She earned her license from the &lt;em&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A9d%C3%A9ration_A%C3%A9ronautique_Internationale" target="_blank"&gt;Fédération Aéronautique Internationale&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/em&gt; on June 15, 1921.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessie_Coleman#cite_note-Women_in_Aviation_International-5" target="_blank"&gt;[5]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessie_Coleman#cite_note-essence-6" target="_blank"&gt;[6]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessie_Coleman#cite_note-phof-11" target="_blank"&gt;[11]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>
      <enclosure url="https://www.patreon.com/api/rss/u/2bweW97Ko-8Fw3yE-z_ORgMzF4fMRqXz/e/150779649.mp3?sig=TkO5batIHuKEsiZJ5EjZqq8Kd_XT0nV8uu9T70sb9hU%3D" length="31778432" type="audio/mpeg"/>

      <title>The Super Bowl</title>
      <link>https://www.patreon.com/posts/super-bowl-149806469</link>
      <itunes:image href="https://c10.patreonusercontent.com/4/patreon-media/p/post/149806469/0ef329205483467e8fd3e51f00e1ec8b/eyJkIjo3MiwiaCI6MzAwMCwic3RyaXBfYWxwaGEiOjEsInciOjMwMDAsIndlYnAiOjB9/1.jpg?token-hash=IcqjNTnar1VYH4nv4HuGrSG7g40Q0ClcNONX0gCEo_Q%3D"/>
      <itunes:episode>460</itunes:episode>
      <itunes:season>1</itunes:season>
      <itunes:duration>2509</itunes:duration>
      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;he &lt;strong&gt;Super Bowl&lt;/strong&gt; is the annual &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_NFL_championship" target="_blank"&gt;league championship&lt;/a&gt; game of the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Football_League" target="_blank"&gt;National Football League&lt;/a&gt; (NFL) of the United States. It has served as the final game of every NFL season since &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1966_NFL_season" target="_blank"&gt;1966&lt;/a&gt;, replacing the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_National_Football_League_championship" target="_blank"&gt;NFL Championship Game&lt;/a&gt;. Since 2022, the game has been played on the second Sunday in February. Prior Super Bowls were played on Sundays in early to mid-January from 1967 to 1978, late January from 1979 to 2003&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Bowl#cite_note-1" target="_blank"&gt;[a]&lt;/a&gt; and the first Sunday of February from 2004 to 2021. Winning teams are awarded the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vince_Lombardi_Trophy" target="_blank"&gt;Vince Lombardi Trophy&lt;/a&gt;, named after the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vince_Lombardi" target="_blank"&gt;eponymous coach&lt;/a&gt; who won the first two Super Bowls. Because the NFL restricts the use of its "Super Bowl" trademark, it is frequently referred to as the "big game" or other generic terms by non-sponsoring corporations. The day the game is held is commonly referred to as "&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Bowl_Sunday" target="_blank"&gt;Super Bowl Sunday&lt;/a&gt;" or "Super Sunday".&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <title>Feral Children</title>
      <link>https://www.patreon.com/posts/feral-children-148052313</link>
      <itunes:image href="https://c10.patreonusercontent.com/4/patreon-media/p/post/148052313/f7e7d1218a5949938a1290ecdda24f51/eyJkIjo3MiwiaCI6MzAwMCwic3RyaXBfYWxwaGEiOjEsInciOjMwMDAsIndlYnAiOjB9/1.jpg?token-hash=eznmsoynGiRHGxTi3sre9-L5D2374TtipfXDvXCUyLs%3D"/>
      <itunes:episode>458</itunes:episode>
      <itunes:duration>1973</itunes:duration>
      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;A &lt;strong&gt;feral child&lt;/strong&gt; (also called &lt;strong&gt;wild child&lt;/strong&gt;) is a young individual who has lived isolated from human contact from a very young age, with little or no experience of human care, social behavior, or language. Such children lack the basics of primary and secondary &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialization" target="_blank"&gt;socialization&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feral_child#cite_note-1" target="_blank"&gt;[1]&lt;/a&gt; The term is used to refer to children who have suffered severe &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_abuse" target="_blank"&gt;abuse&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychological_trauma" target="_blank"&gt;trauma&lt;/a&gt; before being abandoned or running away. They are sometimes the subjects of &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folklore" target="_blank"&gt;folklore&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legend" target="_blank"&gt;legends&lt;/a&gt;, often portrayed as having been raised by animals. While there are many cases of children being found in proximity to wild animals, there are no eyewitness accounts of animals feeding human children.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feral_child#cite_note-2" target="_blank"&gt;[2]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>
      <enclosure url="https://www.patreon.com/api/rss/u/2bweW97Ko-8Fw3yE-z_ORgMzF4fMRqXz/e/148052313.mp3?sig=VQeoG65EmWZ0Df6xPe_hDU5B5FKevhhFLo2DxCbwqes%3D" length="31552640" type="audio/mpeg"/>

    <item>
      <title>Failed Constitutional Amendments</title>
      <link>https://www.patreon.com/posts/failed-148051908</link>
      <itunes:image href="https://c10.patreonusercontent.com/4/patreon-media/p/post/148051908/6f770cc5dd4c41a4a66344477a1ba1c1/eyJkIjo3MiwiaCI6MzAwMCwic3RyaXBfYWxwaGEiOjEsInciOjMwMDAsIndlYnAiOjB9/1.jpg?token-hash=o-3s9Tugnwk7-9SxEDRakhdZT0RUcBOe96VCV-ZK4I4%3D"/>
      <itunes:episode>457</itunes:episode>
      <itunes:duration>2537</itunes:duration>
      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;Hundreds of proposed &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_amendments_to_the_United_States_Constitution" target="_blank"&gt;amendments&lt;/a&gt; to the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Constitution" target="_blank"&gt;United States Constitution&lt;/a&gt; are introduced during each session of the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Congress" target="_blank"&gt;United States Congress&lt;/a&gt;. From 1789 through January 3, 2025, approximately 11,985 measures have been proposed to amend the United States Constitution.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_proposed_amendments_to_the_Constitution_of_the_United_States#cite_note-1" target="_blank"&gt;[1]&lt;/a&gt; Collectively, members of the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives" target="_blank"&gt;House&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate" target="_blank"&gt;Senate&lt;/a&gt; typically propose around 200 amendments during each two-year term of &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Congress" target="_blank"&gt;Congress&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_proposed_amendments_to_the_Constitution_of_the_United_States#cite_note-C-SPAN's_Capitol_Questions-2" target="_blank"&gt;[2]&lt;/a&gt; Most, however, never get out of the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_congressional_committee" target="_blank"&gt;Congressional committees&lt;/a&gt; in which they were proposed. Only a fraction of those actually receive enough support to win Congressional approval to go through the constitutional ratification process. Some proposed amendments are introduced over and over again in different sessions of Congress. It is also common for a number of identical resolutions to be offered on issues that have widespread public and congressional support&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captain_(British_Army_and_Royal_Marines)" target="_blank"&gt;Captain&lt;/a&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Sir Thomas Moore&lt;/strong&gt; (30 April 1920 – 2 February 2021), more popularly known as &lt;strong&gt;Captain Tom&lt;/strong&gt;, was a &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Army" target="_blank"&gt;British Army&lt;/a&gt; officer and &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fundraiser" target="_blank"&gt;fundraiser&lt;/a&gt;. He made international headlines in April 2020 when he raised money for charity in the run-up to his &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centenarian#United_Kingdom_and_Ireland" target="_blank"&gt;100th birthday&lt;/a&gt; during the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_United_Kingdom" target="_blank"&gt;COVID-19 pandemic&lt;/a&gt;. He &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India_in_World_War_II" target="_blank"&gt;served in India&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burma_campaign" target="_blank"&gt;Burma campaign&lt;/a&gt; during the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War" target="_blank"&gt;Second World War&lt;/a&gt;, and later became an instructor in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armoured_warfare" target="_blank"&gt;armoured warfare&lt;/a&gt;. After the war, he worked as managing director of a concrete company and was an avid motorcycle racer.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;On 6 April 2020, at the age of 99 during the first COVID-19 national lockdown, Moore began to walk 100 lengths of his garden in aid of &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NHS_Charities_Together" target="_blank"&gt;NHS Charities Together&lt;/a&gt;, with the goal of raising £1,000 by his 100th birthday on 30 April. In the 24-day course of his fundraising, he made many media appearances and became a household name in the UK, earning a number of accolades and attracting over 1.5 million individual donations.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

The tale is about a &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fir_tree" target="_blank"&gt;fir tree&lt;/a&gt; so anxious to grow up, so anxious for greater things, that he cannot appreciate living in the moment. The tale was first published 21 December 1844 with "&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Snow_Queen" target="_blank"&gt;The Snow Queen&lt;/a&gt;", in &lt;em&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Fairy_Tales._First_Volume#New_Fairy_Tales._First_Volume._Second_Collection" target="_blank"&gt;New Fairy Tales. First Volume. Second Collection&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/em&gt;, in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copenhagen,_Denmark" target="_blank"&gt;Copenhagen, Denmark&lt;/a&gt;, by C.A. Reitzel. One scholar (Andersen biographer &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Jackie_Wullschlager&amp;amp;action=edit&amp;amp;redlink=1" target="_blank"&gt;Jackie Wullschlager&lt;/a&gt; [&lt;a href="https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackie_Wullschlager" target="_blank"&gt;de&lt;/a&gt;]) indicates that "The Fir-Tree" was the first of Andersen's fairy tales to express a deep pessimism.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fir-Tree#cite_note-Wullschlager-1" target="_blank"&gt;[1]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;During the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_space_exploration#Pre-20th_century" target="_blank"&gt;late 19th and early 20th centuries&lt;/a&gt;, it was erroneously believed that there were "&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal" target="_blank"&gt;canals&lt;/a&gt;" on the planet &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mars" target="_blank"&gt;Mars&lt;/a&gt;. These were a network of long straight lines in the equatorial regions from 60° north to 60° south latitude on Mars, observed by &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomer" target="_blank"&gt;astronomers&lt;/a&gt; using early telescopes without photography.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;They were first described by the Italian astronomer &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giovanni_Schiaparelli" target="_blank"&gt;Giovanni Schiaparelli&lt;/a&gt; during the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_(astronomy)" target="_blank"&gt;opposition&lt;/a&gt; of 1877, and attested to by later observers. Schiaparelli called these &lt;em&gt;canali&lt;/em&gt; ("&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_(geography)" target="_blank"&gt;channels&lt;/a&gt;"), which was mistranslated into English as "canals". The Irish astronomer &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_E._Burton" target="_blank"&gt;Charles E. Burton&lt;/a&gt; made some of the earliest drawings of straight-line features on Mars, although his drawings did not match Schiaparelli's.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Balloonfest '86&lt;/strong&gt; was a &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fundraising" target="_blank"&gt;fundraising&lt;/a&gt; event in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland" target="_blank"&gt;Cleveland&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio" target="_blank"&gt;Ohio&lt;/a&gt;, United States, held on September 27, 1986, in which the local chapter of &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Way" target="_blank"&gt;United Way&lt;/a&gt; set a &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_record" target="_blank"&gt;world record&lt;/a&gt; by &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balloon_release" target="_blank"&gt;releasing&lt;/a&gt; almost 1.5 million &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balloon" target="_blank"&gt;balloons&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balloonfest_%2786#cite_note-summer-2" target="_blank"&gt;[2]&lt;/a&gt; The event was intended to be a harmless &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Publicity_stunt" target="_blank"&gt;publicity stunt&lt;/a&gt;. However, the released balloons drifted back over the city and &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Erie" target="_blank"&gt;Lake Erie&lt;/a&gt; and landed in the surrounding area, causing problems for traffic and a nearby airport.       <title>"Rube" Waddell </title>

      <itunes:image href="https://c10.patreonusercontent.com/4/patreon-media/p/post/139479573/cf528ab547534f30bb8868245a9d86f6/eyJkIjo3MiwiaCI6MzAwMCwic3RyaXBfYWxwaGEiOjEsInciOjMwMDAsIndlYnAiOjB9/1.jpg?token-hash=uF_DqS3gUMDWz7fs2qqqzcKY5Ts8e7K0sFCndWIyCwE%3D"/>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;George Edward&lt;/strong&gt; "&lt;strong&gt;Rube&lt;/strong&gt;" &lt;strong&gt;Waddell&lt;/strong&gt; (October 13, 1876 – April 1, 1914) was an American &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pitcher" target="_blank"&gt;pitcher&lt;/a&gt; in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_League_Baseball" target="_blank"&gt;Major League Baseball&lt;/a&gt; (MLB). A &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left-hander" target="_blank"&gt;left-hander&lt;/a&gt;, he played for 13 years, with the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisville_Colonels" target="_blank"&gt;Louisville Colonels&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pittsburgh_Pirates" target="_blank"&gt;Pittsburgh Pirates&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Orphans" target="_blank"&gt;Chicago Orphans&lt;/a&gt; in the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_League_(baseball)" target="_blank"&gt;National League&lt;/a&gt;, as well as the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Athletics" target="_blank"&gt;Philadelphia Athletics&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Louis_Browns" target="_blank"&gt;St. Louis Browns&lt;/a&gt; in the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_League" target="_blank"&gt;American League&lt;/a&gt;. Born in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bradford,_Pennsylvania" target="_blank"&gt;Bradford, Pennsylvania&lt;/a&gt;, and raised in Prospect, Pennsylvania, Waddell was elected to the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Baseball_Hall_of_Fame_and_Museum" target="_blank"&gt;Baseball Hall of Fame&lt;/a&gt; in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1946_Baseball_Hall_of_Fame_balloting" target="_blank"&gt;1946&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Waddell is best remembered for his highly &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eccentricity_(behavior)" target="_blank"&gt;eccentric&lt;/a&gt; behavior, and for being a remarkably dominant strikeout pitcher in an era when batters were expert at making contact. He had an excellent &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fastball" target="_blank"&gt;fastball&lt;/a&gt;, a sharp-breaking &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curveball" target="_blank"&gt;curveball&lt;/a&gt;, a &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Screwball" target="_blank"&gt;screwball&lt;/a&gt;, and superb control; his strikeout-to-walk ratio was almost 3-to-1, and he led the major leagues in strikeouts for six consecutive years.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Mummia&lt;/strong&gt;, &lt;strong&gt;mumia&lt;/strong&gt;, or originally &lt;strong&gt;mummy&lt;/strong&gt; referred to several different preparations in the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_medicine" target="_blank"&gt;history of medicine&lt;/a&gt;, from "mineral &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pitch_(resin)" target="_blank"&gt;pitch&lt;/a&gt;" to "powdered human &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mummies" target="_blank"&gt;mummies&lt;/a&gt;". It originated from &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic" target="_blank"&gt;Arabic&lt;/a&gt; &lt;em&gt;mūmiyā&lt;/em&gt; "a type of resinous &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitumen" target="_blank"&gt;bitumen&lt;/a&gt; found in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Asia" target="_blank"&gt;Western Asia&lt;/a&gt; and used curatively" in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicine_in_the_medieval_Islamic_world" target="_blank"&gt;traditional Islamic medicine&lt;/a&gt;, which was translated as &lt;em&gt;pissasphaltus&lt;/em&gt; (from "pitch" and "asphalt") in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_medicine" target="_blank"&gt;ancient Greek medicine&lt;/a&gt;. In &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_medicine_of_Western_Europe" target="_blank"&gt;medieval European medicine&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;em&gt;mūmiyā&lt;/em&gt; "bitumen" was &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transliterated" target="_blank"&gt;transliterated&lt;/a&gt; into Latin as &lt;em&gt;mumia&lt;/em&gt; meaning both "a bituminous medicine from Persia" and "mummy". Merchants in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apothecaries" target="_blank"&gt;apothecaries&lt;/a&gt; dispensed expensive mummia bitumen, which was thought to be an effective &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cure-all" target="_blank"&gt;cure-all&lt;/a&gt; for many ailments. It was also used as an &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aphrodisiac" target="_blank"&gt;aphrodisiac&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>
      <enclosure url="https://www.patreon.com/api/rss/u/2bweW97Ko-8Fw3yE-z_ORgMzF4fMRqXz/e/138458917.mp3?sig=rzTVhBRpzZ5NhWeJJzeBSplvBYhixSnNbpJ4LRVLT0o%3D" length="31217792" type="audio/mpeg"/>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Vasa&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/em&gt; (previously &lt;em&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Wasa&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/em&gt;) (Swedish pronunciation: &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Swedish" target="_blank"&gt;[²vɑːsa]&lt;/a&gt; &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Sv-Vasa.ogg" target="_blank"&gt;ⓘ&lt;/a&gt;) is a Swedish &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warship" target="_blank"&gt;warship&lt;/a&gt; built between 1626 and 1628. The ship sank after sailing roughly 1,300 m (1,400 yd) into her maiden voyage on 10 August 1628. She fell into obscurity after most of her valuable &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze" target="_blank"&gt;bronze&lt;/a&gt; &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannon" target="_blank"&gt;cannons&lt;/a&gt; were salvaged in the 17th century, until she was located again in the late 1950s in a busy shipping area in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stockholm" target="_blank"&gt;Stockholm&lt;/a&gt; harbor. The ship was salvaged with a largely intact hull in 1961. She was housed in a temporary museum called Wasavarvet ("The Vasa Shipyard") until 1988 and then moved permanently to the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasa_Museum" target="_blank"&gt;Vasa Museum&lt;/a&gt; in the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_National_City_Park" target="_blank"&gt;Royal National City Park&lt;/a&gt; in Stockholm. Between her recovery in 1961 and the beginning of 2025, &lt;em&gt;Vasa&lt;/em&gt; has been seen by over 45 million visitors.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasa_(ship)#cite_note-2" target="_blank"&gt;[2]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Olestra&lt;/strong&gt; (also known by its brand name &lt;strong&gt;Olean&lt;/strong&gt;) is a &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fat_substitute" target="_blank"&gt;fat substitute&lt;/a&gt; &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_additive" target="_blank"&gt;food additive&lt;/a&gt; that adds no metabolizable calories to products. It has been used in the preparation of otherwise high-fat foods, thereby lowering or eliminating their fat content.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_and_Drug_Administration" target="_blank"&gt;Food and Drug Administration&lt;/a&gt; (FDA) approved olestra for use in the US as a replacement for fats and oils in prepackaged ready-to-eat snacks in 1996,&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olestra#cite_note-AccessData-2" target="_blank"&gt;[2]&lt;/a&gt; concluding that such use "meets the safety standard for food additives, reasonable certainty of no harm".&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olestra#cite_note-FedReg-3" target="_blank"&gt;[3]&lt;/a&gt;: 46399  In the early 2000s, olestra lost popularity due to supposed side effects and is largely phased out, but products containing the ingredient are available in some countries.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pluto&lt;/strong&gt; (&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minor-planet_designation" target="_blank"&gt;minor-planet designation&lt;/a&gt;: &lt;strong&gt;134340 Pluto&lt;/strong&gt;) is a &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwarf_planet" target="_blank"&gt;dwarf planet&lt;/a&gt; in the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuiper_belt" target="_blank"&gt;Kuiper belt&lt;/a&gt;, a ring of &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Neptunian_object" target="_blank"&gt;bodies beyond the orbit of Neptune&lt;/a&gt;. It is the ninth-largest and tenth-most-massive known object to directly orbit the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun" target="_blank"&gt;Sun&lt;/a&gt;. It is the largest known &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Neptunian_object" target="_blank"&gt;trans-Neptunian object&lt;/a&gt; by volume by a small margin, but is less massive than &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eris_(dwarf_planet)" target="_blank"&gt;Eris&lt;/a&gt;. Like other Kuiper belt objects, Pluto is made primarily of ice and rock and is much smaller than the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_planet" target="_blank"&gt;inner planets&lt;/a&gt;. Pluto has roughly one-sixth the mass of the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon" target="_blank"&gt;Moon&lt;/a&gt; and one-third its volume. Originally considered a planet, its classification was changed when astronomers adopted a new &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Definition_of_planet" target="_blank"&gt;definition of &lt;/a&gt;&lt;em&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Definition_of_planet" target="_blank"&gt;planet&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/em&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Defenestration&lt;/strong&gt; (from &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Latin" target="_blank"&gt;Neo-Latin&lt;/a&gt; &lt;em&gt;de&lt;/em&gt; &lt;em&gt;fenestrā&lt;/em&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defenestration#cite_note-1" target="_blank"&gt;[1]&lt;/a&gt;) is the act of throwing someone or something out of a &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Window" target="_blank"&gt;window&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defenestration#cite_note-2" target="_blank"&gt;[2]&lt;/a&gt; The term was coined around the time of an &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defenestrations_of_Prague#The_1618_Defenestration_of_Prague" target="_blank"&gt;incident&lt;/a&gt; in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prague_Castle" target="_blank"&gt;Prague Castle&lt;/a&gt; in the year 1618 which became the spark that started the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirty_Years%27_War" target="_blank"&gt;Thirty Years' War&lt;/a&gt;. This was done in "good Bohemian style", referring to the defenestration which had occurred in Prague's &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Town_Hall_(Prague)" target="_blank"&gt;New Town Hall&lt;/a&gt; almost 200 years earlier (July 1419), and on that occasion led to the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussite_war" target="_blank"&gt;Hussite war&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defenestration#cite_note-3" target="_blank"&gt;[3]&lt;/a&gt; The word comes from the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Latin" target="_blank"&gt;Neo-Latin&lt;/a&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defenestration#cite_note-4" target="_blank"&gt;[4]&lt;/a&gt; &lt;em&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/de-" target="_blank"&gt;de-&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/em&gt; (&lt;em&gt;down from&lt;/em&gt;) and &lt;em&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/fenestra" target="_blank"&gt;fenestra&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/em&gt; (window or opening).&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defenestration#cite_note-5" target="_blank"&gt;[5]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;By extension, the term is also used to describe the forcible or &lt;a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/summary" target="_blank"&gt;summary&lt;/a&gt; removal of an adversary.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defenestration#cite_note-6" target="_blank"&gt;[6]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Moving Day&lt;/strong&gt; was a tradition in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_City" target="_blank"&gt;New York City&lt;/a&gt; dating back to &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_America" target="_blank"&gt;colonial&lt;/a&gt; times and lasting until after &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II" target="_blank"&gt;World War II&lt;/a&gt;. On February 1, sometimes known as "Rent Day", landlords would give notice to their tenants what the new rent would be after the end of the quarter,&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moving_Day_(New_York_City)#cite_note-1" target="_blank"&gt;[1]&lt;/a&gt; and the tenants would spend good-weather days in the early spring searching for new houses and the best deals.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moving_Day_(New_York_City)#cite_note-2" target="_blank"&gt;[2]&lt;/a&gt; On &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_1" target="_blank"&gt;May 1&lt;/a&gt;,&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moving_Day_(New_York_City)#cite_note-3" target="_blank"&gt;[3]&lt;/a&gt; all leases in the city expired simultaneously at 9:00 am, causing thousands of people to &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moving_(address)" target="_blank"&gt;change their residences&lt;/a&gt;, all at the same time.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moving_Day_(New_York_City)#cite_note-4" target="_blank"&gt;[4]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moving_Day_(New_York_City)#cite_note-5" target="_blank"&gt;[5]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Samuel Langhorne Clemens&lt;/strong&gt; (November 30, 1835 – April 21, 1910), known by the pen name &lt;strong&gt;Mark Twain&lt;/strong&gt;, was an American writer, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humorist" target="_blank"&gt;humorist&lt;/a&gt;, and essayist. He was praised as the "greatest humorist the United States has produced",&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Twain#cite_note-1" target="_blank"&gt;[1]&lt;/a&gt; with &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Faulkner" target="_blank"&gt;William Faulkner&lt;/a&gt; calling him "the father of &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_literature" target="_blank"&gt;American literature&lt;/a&gt;".&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Twain#cite_note-faulkner-2" target="_blank"&gt;[2]&lt;/a&gt; Twain's novels include &lt;em&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Adventures_of_Tom_Sawyer" target="_blank"&gt;The Adventures of Tom Sawyer&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/em&gt; (1876) and its sequel, &lt;em&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adventures_of_Huckleberry_Finn" target="_blank"&gt;Adventures of Huckleberry Finn&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/em&gt; (1884),&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Twain#cite_note-3" target="_blank"&gt;[3]&lt;/a&gt; with the latter often called the "&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_American_Novel" target="_blank"&gt;Great American Novel&lt;/a&gt;". He also wrote &lt;em&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Connecticut_Yankee_in_King_Arthur%27s_Court" target="_blank"&gt;A Connecticut Yankee in King Arthur's Court&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/em&gt; (1889) and &lt;em&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pudd%27nhead_Wilson" target="_blank"&gt;Pudd'nhead Wilson&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/em&gt; (1894) and cowrote &lt;em&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Gilded_Age:_A_Tale_of_Today" target="_blank"&gt;The Gilded Age: A Tale of Today&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/em&gt; (1873) with &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Dudley_Warner" target="_blank"&gt;Charles Dudley Warner&lt;/a&gt;. The novelist &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_Hemingway" target="_blank"&gt;Ernest Hemingway&lt;/a&gt; claimed that "All modern American literature comes from one book by Mark Twain called &lt;em&gt;Huckleberry Finn&lt;/em&gt;."&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Optography&lt;/strong&gt; is the process of viewing or retrieving an &lt;strong&gt;optogram&lt;/strong&gt;, an image on the retina of the eye. A belief that the eye "recorded" the last image seen before death was widespread in the late 19th and early 20th centuries, and was a frequent plot device in fiction of the time, to the extent that police photographed the victims' eyes in several real-life murder investigations, in case the theory was true.       <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;The &lt;strong&gt;Charge of the Light Brigade&lt;/strong&gt; was a military action undertaken by British &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_cavalry" target="_blank"&gt;light cavalry&lt;/a&gt; against Russian forces during the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Balaclava" target="_blank"&gt;Battle of Balaclava&lt;/a&gt; in the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_War" target="_blank"&gt;Crimean War&lt;/a&gt;, resulting in many casualties to the cavalry. On 25 October 1854, the Light Brigade, led by &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Brudenell,_7th_Earl_of_Cardigan" target="_blank"&gt;Lord Cardigan&lt;/a&gt;, mounted a frontal assault against a Russian artillery battery which was well-prepared with excellent fields of defensive fire. The charge was the result of a misunderstood order from the commander-in-chief, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FitzRoy_Somerset,_1st_Baron_Raglan" target="_blank"&gt;Lord Raglan&lt;/a&gt;, who had intended the Light Brigade to attack a different objective for which light cavalry was better suited, to prevent the Russians from removing captured guns from overrun Turkish positions. The Light Brigade made its charge under withering direct fire and reached its target, scattering some of the gunners, but was forced to retreat immediately.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <itunes:image href="https://c10.patreonusercontent.com/4/patreon-media/p/post/129382934/fba61111df524bfc97c6a990ffc03558/eyJkIjo3MiwiaCI6MzAwMCwic3RyaXBfYWxwaGEiOjEsInciOjMwMDAsIndlYnAiOjB9/1.jpg?token-hash=VMdWecuLKf9fYB-mJlJ6P-RshBOTTBLuU0a7vHmdng0%3D"/>
      <itunes:explicit>true</itunes:explicit>
      <itunes:episode>423</itunes:episode>
      <itunes:episodeType>full</itunes:episodeType>
      <itunes:duration>2029</itunes:duration>
      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fax&lt;/strong&gt; (short for &lt;strong&gt;facsimile&lt;/strong&gt;), sometimes called &lt;strong&gt;telecopying&lt;/strong&gt; or &lt;strong&gt;telefax&lt;/strong&gt; (short for &lt;strong&gt;telefacsimile&lt;/strong&gt;), is the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telephone" target="_blank"&gt;telephonic&lt;/a&gt; transmission of scanned printed material (both text and images), normally to a telephone number connected to a printer or other output device. The original document is scanned with a &lt;strong&gt;fax machine&lt;/strong&gt; (or a &lt;strong&gt;telecopier&lt;/strong&gt;), which processes the contents (text or images) as a single fixed graphic image, converting it into a &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitmap" target="_blank"&gt;bitmap&lt;/a&gt;, and then transmitting it through the telephone system in the form of audio-frequency tones. The receiving fax machine interprets the tones and reconstructs the image, printing a paper copy.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fax#cite_note-1" target="_blank"&gt;[1]&lt;/a&gt; Early systems used direct conversions of image darkness to audio tone in a continuous or analog manner. Since the 1980s, most machines transmit an audio-encoded digital representation of the page, using &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_compression" target="_blank"&gt;data compression&lt;/a&gt; to transmit areas that are all-white or all-black, more quickly.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;The &lt;strong&gt;Moon&lt;/strong&gt; is &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth" target="_blank"&gt;Earth&lt;/a&gt;'s only &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_satellite" target="_blank"&gt;natural satellite&lt;/a&gt;. It &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbit_of_the_Moon" target="_blank"&gt;orbits&lt;/a&gt; Earth at &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_distance" target="_blank"&gt;an average distance&lt;/a&gt; of 384399 km (238,854 mi; about 30 times &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth_diameter" target="_blank"&gt;Earth's diameter&lt;/a&gt;). The Moon's orbital period (&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_month#Synodic_month" target="_blank"&gt;lunar month&lt;/a&gt;) and rotation period (&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_day" target="_blank"&gt;lunar day&lt;/a&gt;) are &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_locking" target="_blank"&gt;synchronized&lt;/a&gt; by Earth's &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_forces" target="_blank"&gt;gravitational pull&lt;/a&gt; at 29.5 Earth days, making &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_side_of_the_Moon" target="_blank"&gt;the same side&lt;/a&gt; of the Moon always face Earth. The Moon's pull on Earth is the main driver of Earth's &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tide" target="_blank"&gt;tides&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <title>Cuban Missile Crisis </title>
      <link>https://www.patreon.com/posts/cuban-missile-125585438</link>
      <itunes:image href="https://c10.patreonusercontent.com/4/patreon-media/p/post/125585438/d6b56f0c7a454c1a973e66ac48d7f873/eyJkIjo3MiwiaCI6MzAwMCwic3RyaXBfYWxwaGEiOjEsInciOjMwMDAsIndlYnAiOjB9/1.jpg?token-hash=Md0QI9NgTh_nlvSvpNP_VkxQiiyYxwcEjNgLFsh8a0o%3D"/>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;The &lt;strong&gt;Cuban Missile Crisis&lt;/strong&gt;, also known as the &lt;strong&gt;October Crisis&lt;/strong&gt; (&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_language" target="_blank"&gt;Spanish&lt;/a&gt;: &lt;em&gt;Crisis de Octubre&lt;/em&gt;) in Cuba, or the &lt;strong&gt;Caribbean Crisis&lt;/strong&gt; (&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language" target="_blank"&gt;Russian&lt;/a&gt;: Карибский кризис, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Russian" target="_blank"&gt;romanized&lt;/a&gt;: &lt;em&gt;Karibskiy krizis&lt;/em&gt;), was a 13-day confrontation between the governments of the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States" target="_blank"&gt;United States&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union" target="_blank"&gt;Soviet Union&lt;/a&gt;, when American deployments of &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_weapons_delivery" target="_blank"&gt;nuclear missiles&lt;/a&gt; in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italy" target="_blank"&gt;Italy&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkey" target="_blank"&gt;Turkey&lt;/a&gt; were matched by Soviet deployments of nuclear missiles in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuba" target="_blank"&gt;Cuba&lt;/a&gt;. The crisis lasted from 16 to 28 October 1962. The confrontation is widely considered &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_nuclear_close_calls" target="_blank"&gt;the closest&lt;/a&gt; the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War" target="_blank"&gt;Cold War&lt;/a&gt; came to escalating into full-scale &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_warfare" target="_blank"&gt;nuclear war&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Missile_Crisis#cite_note-1" target="_blank"&gt;[1]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>
      <enclosure url="https://www.patreon.com/api/rss/u/2bweW97Ko-8Fw3yE-z_ORgMzF4fMRqXz/e/125585438.mp3?sig=lPDfUXVFpfkIqr-i1b942CKMVOMo9X_dIDgGJv9P1us%3D" length="38723840" type="audio/mpeg"/>

Other OSS functions included the use of propaganda, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subversion" target="_blank"&gt;subversion&lt;/a&gt;, and post-war planning.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Audie Leon Murphy&lt;/strong&gt; (20 June 1925 – 28 May 1971)&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Audie_Murphy#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith20151,_12%E2%80%9313-1" target="_blank"&gt;[1]&lt;/a&gt; was an American soldier, actor, and songwriter. He was widely celebrated as the most decorated American combat soldier of &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II" target="_blank"&gt;World War II&lt;/a&gt;,&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Audie_Murphy#cite_note-6" target="_blank"&gt;[4]&lt;/a&gt; and has been described as the most highly decorated enlisted soldier in U.S. history.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Audie_Murphy#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraham1989101-7" target="_blank"&gt;[5]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Audie_Murphy#cite_note-8" target="_blank"&gt;[6]&lt;/a&gt; He received every military combat award for valor available from the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Army" target="_blank"&gt;United States Army&lt;/a&gt;, as well as French and Belgian awards for heroism. His political and religious teachings were well known in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magna_Graecia" target="_blank"&gt;Magna Graecia&lt;/a&gt; and influenced the philosophies of &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato" target="_blank"&gt;Plato&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle" target="_blank"&gt;Aristotle&lt;/a&gt;, and, through them, the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_philosophy" target="_blank"&gt;West&lt;/a&gt; in general. Knowledge of his life is clouded by legend; modern scholars disagree regarding Pythagoras's education and influences, but they do agree that, around 530 BC, he travelled to &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crotone" target="_blank"&gt;Croton&lt;/a&gt; in southern Italy, where he founded a school in which initiates were sworn to secrecy and lived a communal, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asceticism" target="_blank"&gt;ascetic&lt;/a&gt; lifestyle.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The earliest known version of the tale is found in the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language" target="_blank"&gt;French&lt;/a&gt; narrative &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perceforest" target="_blank"&gt;Perceforest&lt;/a&gt;, written between 1330 and 1344.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sleeping_Beauty#cite_note-8" target="_blank"&gt;[7]&lt;/a&gt; Another was the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_language" target="_blank"&gt;Catalan&lt;/a&gt; poem &lt;em&gt;Frayre de Joy e Sor de Paser&lt;/em&gt;.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sleeping_Beauty#cite_note-9" target="_blank"&gt;[8]&lt;/a&gt; &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giambattista_Basile" target="_blank"&gt;Giambattista Basile&lt;/a&gt; wrote another, "&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun,_Moon,_and_Talia" target="_blank"&gt;Sun, Moon, and Talia&lt;/a&gt;" for his collection &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentamerone" target="_blank"&gt;Pentamerone&lt;/a&gt;, published posthumously in 1634–36&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sleeping_Beauty#cite_note-Folk_&amp;amp;_Fairy_Tales-10" target="_blank"&gt;[9]&lt;/a&gt; and adapted by &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Perrault" target="_blank"&gt;Charles Perrault&lt;/a&gt; in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Histoires_ou_contes_du_temps_pass%C3%A9" target="_blank"&gt;Histoires ou contes du temps passé&lt;/a&gt; in 1697. The version collected and printed by the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brothers_Grimm" target="_blank"&gt;Brothers Grimm&lt;/a&gt; was one orally transmitted from the Perrault version,&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sleeping_Beauty#cite_note-11" target="_blank"&gt;[10]&lt;/a&gt; while including own attributes like the thorny rose hedge and the curse.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sleeping_Beauty#cite_note-12" target="_blank"&gt;[11]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Sun, Moon, and Talia&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/em&gt; (&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_language" target="_blank"&gt;Italian&lt;/a&gt;: &lt;em&gt;Sole, Luna, e Talia&lt;/em&gt;) is an Italian &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literary_fairy_tale" target="_blank"&gt;literary fairy tale&lt;/a&gt; written by &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giambattista_Basile" target="_blank"&gt;Giambattista Basile&lt;/a&gt; and published posthumously in the last volume of his 1634-36 work, the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentamerone" target="_blank"&gt;Pentamerone&lt;/a&gt;. &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Perrault" target="_blank"&gt;Charles Perrault&lt;/a&gt; retold this fairy tale in 1697 as &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sleeping_Beauty" target="_blank"&gt;Sleeping Beauty&lt;/a&gt;, as did the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brothers_Grimm" target="_blank"&gt;Brothers Grimm&lt;/a&gt; in 1812 as &lt;em&gt;Little Briar Rose&lt;/em&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <title>The Sinking of the Whaleship Essex</title>
      <link>https://www.patreon.com/posts/sinking-of-essex-116254233</link>
      <itunes:image href="https://c10.patreonusercontent.com/4/patreon-media/p/post/116254233/b98b5e03528f46e581297cb8af0b5309/eyJkIjo3MiwiaCI6MzAwMCwic3RyaXBfYWxwaGEiOjEsInciOjMwMDAsIndlYnAiOjB9/1.jpg?token-hash=h2zuc-1uVXqABkvuQpn-xGEddPiCkRBWJjCcX9PzX74%3D"/>
      <itunes:explicit>true</itunes:explicit>
      <itunes:episode>397</itunes:episode>
      <itunes:duration>2274</itunes:duration>
      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Essex&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/em&gt; was an American &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whaler" target="_blank"&gt;whaling ship&lt;/a&gt; from &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nantucket" target="_blank"&gt;Nantucket&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts" target="_blank"&gt;Massachusetts&lt;/a&gt;, which was launched in 1799. On November 20, 1820, while at sea in the southern &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Ocean" target="_blank"&gt;Pacific Ocean&lt;/a&gt; under the command of Captain &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Pollard_Jr." target="_blank"&gt;George Pollard Jr.&lt;/a&gt;, the ship was attacked and sunk by a &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sperm_whale" target="_blank"&gt;sperm whale&lt;/a&gt;. About 2,000 nautical miles (3,700 km) from the coast of South America, the 20-man crew was forced to make for land in three &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whaleboat" target="_blank"&gt;whaleboats&lt;/a&gt; with what food and water they could salvage from the wreck.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;After a month at sea the crew landed on the uninhabited &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henderson_Island_(Pitcairn_Islands)" target="_blank"&gt;Henderson Island&lt;/a&gt;. Three men elected to stay on the island, from which they were rescued in April 1821, while the remaining seventeen set off again for the coast of South America. The men suffered severe &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dehydration" target="_blank"&gt;dehydration&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starvation" target="_blank"&gt;starvation&lt;/a&gt; and exposure on the open ocean, and the survivors eventually resorted to &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_cannibalism" target="_blank"&gt;cannibalism&lt;/a&gt;. By the time they were rescued in February 1821, three months after the sinking of &lt;em&gt;Essex&lt;/em&gt;, only five of the seventeen were alive.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;First mate &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Owen_Chase" target="_blank"&gt;Owen Chase&lt;/a&gt; and cabin boy &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Nickerson" target="_blank"&gt;Thomas Nickerson&lt;/a&gt; later wrote accounts of the ordeal. The tragedy attracted international attention, and inspired &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herman_Melville" target="_blank"&gt;Herman Melville&lt;/a&gt; to write his 1851 novel, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moby-Dick" target="_blank"&gt;Moby-Dick&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>
      <enclosure url="https://www.patreon.com/api/rss/u/2bweW97Ko-8Fw3yE-z_ORgMzF4fMRqXz/e/116254233.mp3?sig=D-T_6CGoTX5y1eY8nJtDDPPU-E16S8GFFHc1mi41x_4%3D" length="54553088" type="audio/mpeg"/>

    <item>
      <title>Pied Piper and  the Children's Crusade</title>
      <link>https://www.patreon.com/posts/pied-piper-and-113547447</link>
      <itunes:image href="https://c10.patreonusercontent.com/4/patreon-media/p/post/113547447/eed65a3d7fc8430c895a982aca45d980/eyJkIjo3MiwiaCI6MzAwMCwic3RyaXBfYWxwaGEiOjEsInciOjMwMDAsIndlYnAiOjB9/1.jpg?token-hash=eSSGlJXoGDQl8dLKlCVdcV5kIqyLc9ivtlmBhhegTSc%3D"/>
      <itunes:duration>2272</itunes:duration>
      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;The legend dates back to the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages" target="_blank"&gt;Middle Ages&lt;/a&gt;. The earliest references describe a piper, dressed in multicoloured ("&lt;a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/pied" target="_blank"&gt;pied&lt;/a&gt;") clothing, who was a rat catcher hired by the town to lure rats away&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pied_Piper_of_Hamelin#cite_note-1" target="_blank"&gt;[1]&lt;/a&gt; with his magic &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pipe_(instrument)" target="_blank"&gt;pipe&lt;/a&gt;. When the citizens refused to pay for this service as promised, he retaliated by using his instrument's magical power on their children, leading them away as he had the rats. This version of the story spread as folklore and has appeared in the writings of &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Wolfgang_von_Goethe" target="_blank"&gt;Johann Wolfgang von Goethe&lt;/a&gt;, the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brothers_Grimm" target="_blank"&gt;Brothers Grimm&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Browning" target="_blank"&gt;Robert Browning&lt;/a&gt;, among others. The phrase "pied piper" has become a metaphor for a person who attracts a following through charisma or false promises.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pied_Piper_of_Hamelin#cite_note-2" target="_blank"&gt;[2]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Anthony Comstock&lt;/strong&gt; (March 7, 1844 – September 21, 1915) was an American anti-vice activist, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Postal_Inspection_Service" target="_blank"&gt;United States Postal Inspector&lt;/a&gt;, and secretary of the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Society_for_the_Suppression_of_Vice" target="_blank"&gt;New York Society for the Suppression of Vice&lt;/a&gt; (NYSSV), who was dedicated to upholding &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_morality" target="_blank"&gt;Christian morality&lt;/a&gt;. He opposed &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obscene" target="_blank"&gt;obscene&lt;/a&gt; literature, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion" target="_blank"&gt;abortion&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_control" target="_blank"&gt;contraception&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masturbation" target="_blank"&gt;masturbation&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gambling" target="_blank"&gt;gambling&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prostitution_in_the_United_States" target="_blank"&gt;prostitution&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patent_medicine" target="_blank"&gt;patent medicine&lt;/a&gt;. The terms &lt;em&gt;comstockery&lt;/em&gt; and &lt;em&gt;comstockism&lt;/em&gt; refer to his extensive &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship" target="_blank"&gt;censorship&lt;/a&gt; campaign of materials that he considered obscene, including &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_control" target="_blank"&gt;birth control&lt;/a&gt; advertised or sent by mail. He used his positions in the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Postal_Service" target="_blank"&gt;U.S. Postal Service&lt;/a&gt; and the NYSSV (in association with the New York police) to make numerous arrests for obscenity and gambling. Besides these pursuits, he was also involved in efforts to suppress &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fraud" target="_blank"&gt;fraudulent&lt;/a&gt; banking schemes, mail swindles, and &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quackery" target="_blank"&gt;medical quackery&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Comstock#cite_note-2" target="_blank"&gt;[2]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

There were also attempts by &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_exile" target="_blank"&gt;Cuban exiles&lt;/a&gt;, sometimes in cooperation with the CIA. The 1975 &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_Committee" target="_blank"&gt;Church Committee&lt;/a&gt; claimed eight proven CIA assassination attempts between 1960 and 1965. In 1976, President &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Ford" target="_blank"&gt;Gerald Ford&lt;/a&gt; issued an Executive Order banning political assassinations. In 2006, Fabián Escalante, former chief of Cuba's intelligence, stated that there had been 634 assassination schemes or attempts. It was sudden, violent, and culturally disruptive for many &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age" target="_blank"&gt;Bronze Age&lt;/a&gt; civilizations, and it brought a sharp economic decline to regional powers, notably ushering in the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Dark_Ages" target="_blank"&gt;Greek Dark Ages&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Bronze_Age_collapse" target="_blank"&gt;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Bronze_Age_collapse&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

On July 2, 1937, Earhart disappeared over the Pacific Ocean while attempting to become the first female pilot to circumnavigate the world. During her life, she embraced celebrity culture and women's rights, and since her disappearance has become a &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_icon" target="_blank"&gt;cultural icon&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amelia_Earhart#cite_note-2" target="_blank"&gt;[2]&lt;/a&gt; Earhart was the first female aviator to fly solo across the Atlantic Ocean and she set many other records.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amelia_Earhart#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOakes1985-3" target="_blank"&gt;[3]&lt;/a&gt; She was one of the first aviators to promote commercial air travel, wrote best-selling books about her flying experiences, and was instrumental in the formation of &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ninety-Nines" target="_blank"&gt;The Ninety-Nines&lt;/a&gt;, an organization for female pilots.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amelia_Earhart#cite_note-FOOTNOTELovell1989152-4" target="_blank"&gt;[4]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

The crater is estimated to be 200 kilometers (120 miles) in diameter and 20 kilometers (12 miles) in depth. It is &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_impact_craters_on_Earth#10_Ma_or_more" target="_blank"&gt;the second largest confirmed impact structure on Earth&lt;/a&gt;, and the only one whose &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peak_ring" target="_blank"&gt;peak ring&lt;/a&gt; is intact and directly accessible for scientific research.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicxulub_crater#cite_note-SchulteOthers2010-4" target="_blank"&gt;[4]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

    <item>
      <title>Operation Eagle Claw</title>
      <link>https://www.patreon.com/posts/operation-eagle-102832656</link>
      <itunes:image href="https://c10.patreonusercontent.com/4/patreon-media/p/post/102832656/c4a80393f8aa42228babfc3375445370/eyJkIjo3MiwiaCI6MzAwMCwic3RyaXBfYWxwaGEiOjEsInciOjMwMDAsIndlYnAiOjB9/1.png?token-hash=CC49ULsevo8JZHBFr6uXXm6h2nGIXnrKV2RUwhZTO34%3D"/>
      <itunes:duration>2145</itunes:duration>
      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Operation Eagle Claw&lt;/strong&gt; was a failed &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_operation" target="_blank"&gt;operation&lt;/a&gt; by the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Armed_Forces" target="_blank"&gt;United States Armed Forces&lt;/a&gt; ordered by &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._President" target="_blank"&gt;U.S. President&lt;/a&gt; &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter" target="_blank"&gt;Jimmy Carter&lt;/a&gt; to attempt the rescue of &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_hostage_crisis" target="_blank"&gt;52 embassy staff held captive&lt;/a&gt; at the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embassy_of_the_United_States,_Tehran" target="_blank"&gt;Embassy of the United States, Tehran&lt;/a&gt; on 24 April 1980.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The operation, one of &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delta_Force" target="_blank"&gt;Delta Force&lt;/a&gt;'s first,&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Eagle_Claw#cite_note-Gabriel106-1" target="_blank"&gt;[1]&lt;/a&gt; encountered many obstacles and failures and was subsequently aborted. Eight helicopters were sent to the first staging area called &lt;em&gt;Desert One&lt;/em&gt;, but only five arrived in operational condition.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Eagle_Claw#cite_note-Atlantic-2" target="_blank"&gt;[2]&lt;/a&gt; One had encountered hydraulic problems, another was caught in a &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haboob" target="_blank"&gt;sand storm&lt;/a&gt;, and the third showed signs of a cracked rotor blade. During the operational planning, it was decided that the mission would be aborted if fewer than six helicopters remained operational upon arrival at the Desert One site, despite only four being absolutely necessary.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Eagle_Claw#cite_note-Atlantic-2" target="_blank"&gt;[2]&lt;/a&gt; In a move that is still discussed in military circles, the field commanders advised President Carter to abort the mission, which he did.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Eagle_Claw#cite_note-3" target="_blank"&gt;[3]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>
      <enclosure url="https://www.patreon.com/api/rss/u/2bweW97Ko-8Fw3yE-z_ORgMzF4fMRqXz/e/102832656.mp3?sig=VI_IYcNxLbyBGHC0Zo6N63JLwBZxi6hSp7AoTjpvtYU%3D" length="34308803" type="audio/mpeg"/>
      <guid isPermaLink="false">102832656</guid>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;The &lt;strong&gt;Battle of Red Cliffs&lt;/strong&gt;, also known as the &lt;strong&gt;Battle of Chibi&lt;/strong&gt;, was a decisive naval battle in the winter of AD 208–209 at the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/End_of_the_Han_dynasty" target="_blank"&gt;end of the Han dynasty&lt;/a&gt;, about twelve years prior to the beginning of the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Kingdoms" target="_blank"&gt;Three Kingdoms&lt;/a&gt; period in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_history" target="_blank"&gt;Chinese history&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Red_Cliffs#cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Crespigny1990264%22The_engagement_at_the_Red_Cliffs_took_place_in_the_winter_of_the_13th_year_of_[[Jian'an_(Eastern_Han)|Jian'an]],_probably_about_the_end_of_208.%22-5" target="_blank"&gt;[4]&lt;/a&gt; The battle was fought between the allied forces of the southern warlords &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun_Quan" target="_blank"&gt;Sun Quan&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liu_Bei" target="_blank"&gt;Liu Bei&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liu_Qi_(Liu_Biao%27s_son)" target="_blank"&gt;Liu Qi&lt;/a&gt; against the numerically superior forces of the northern warlord &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cao_Cao" target="_blank"&gt;Cao Cao&lt;/a&gt;. Liu Bei and Sun Quan frustrated Cao Cao's effort to conquer the land south of the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yangtze_River" target="_blank"&gt;Yangtze River&lt;/a&gt; and reunite the territory of the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Han_dynasty" target="_blank"&gt;Eastern Han dynasty&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;The &lt;strong&gt;United East India Company&lt;/strong&gt; (&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language" target="_blank"&gt;Dutch&lt;/a&gt;: &lt;em&gt;Verenigde Oostindische Compagnie&lt;/em&gt; &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Dutch" target="_blank"&gt;[vərˈeːnɪɣdə oːstˈɪndisə kɔmpɑˈɲi]&lt;/a&gt;, abbreviated as &lt;strong&gt;VOC&lt;/strong&gt;, Dutch: &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Dutch" target="_blank"&gt;[veː.oːˈseː]&lt;/a&gt;) and commonly known as the &lt;strong&gt;Dutch East India Company&lt;/strong&gt;, was a &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chartered_company" target="_blank"&gt;chartered&lt;/a&gt; &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trading_company" target="_blank"&gt;trading company&lt;/a&gt; and the first &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint-stock_company" target="_blank"&gt;joint-stock company&lt;/a&gt; in the world.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company#cite_note-6" target="_blank"&gt;[2]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company#cite_note-7" target="_blank"&gt;[3]&lt;/a&gt; Established on 20 March 1602&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company#cite_note-8" target="_blank"&gt;[4]&lt;/a&gt; by the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States_General_of_the_Netherlands" target="_blank"&gt;States General of the Netherlands&lt;/a&gt; amalgamating &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voorcompagnie" target="_blank"&gt;existing companies&lt;/a&gt;, it was granted a 21-year &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monopoly" target="_blank"&gt;monopoly&lt;/a&gt; to carry out trade activities in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia" target="_blank"&gt;Asia&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company#cite_note-9" target="_blank"&gt;[5]&lt;/a&gt; Shares in the company could be purchased by any citizen of the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Republic" target="_blank"&gt;United Provinces (Dutch Republic)&lt;/a&gt; and then subsequently bought and sold in open-air &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_markets" target="_blank"&gt;secondary markets&lt;/a&gt; (one of which became the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amsterdam_Stock_Exchange" target="_blank"&gt;Amsterdam Stock Exchange&lt;/a&gt;).&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company#cite_note-10" target="_blank"&gt;[6]&lt;/a&gt; The company possessed quasi-governmental powers, including the ability to wage war, imprison and execute convicts,&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company#cite_note-11" target="_blank"&gt;[7]&lt;/a&gt; negotiate treaties, strike &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coinage_of_the_Dutch_East_India_Company" target="_blank"&gt;its own coins&lt;/a&gt;, and establish colonies.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company#cite_note-12" target="_blank"&gt;[8]&lt;/a&gt; Also, because it traded across multiple colonies and countries from both the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_East" target="_blank"&gt;East&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_world" target="_blank"&gt;West&lt;/a&gt;, the VOC is sometimes considered to have been the world's first &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multinational_corporation" target="_blank"&gt;multinational corporation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company#cite_note-13" target="_blank"&gt;[9]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company#cite_note-14" target="_blank"&gt;[10]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <title>1769 transit of Venus</title>
      <link>https://www.patreon.com/posts/1769-transit-of-97907168</link>
      <itunes:image href="https://c10.patreonusercontent.com/4/patreon-media/p/post/97907168/60e189bdd4f4460fbb3e5dfd5f828586/eyJkIjo3MiwiaCI6MzAwMCwic3RyaXBfYWxwaGEiOjEsInciOjMwMDAsIndlYnAiOjB9/1.png?token-hash=DnCYhIQ2URfchOKCQi5SDdQHU4F1wmg91O7hZXmCBxo%3D"/>
      <itunes:duration>2062</itunes:duration>
      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;A &lt;strong&gt;transit of Venus&lt;/strong&gt; across the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun" target="_blank"&gt;Sun&lt;/a&gt; takes place when the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planet" target="_blank"&gt;planet&lt;/a&gt; &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venus" target="_blank"&gt;Venus&lt;/a&gt; passes directly between the Sun and a &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inferior_and_superior_planets" target="_blank"&gt;superior planet&lt;/a&gt;, becoming visible against (and hence obscuring a small portion of) the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_disk" target="_blank"&gt;solar disk&lt;/a&gt;. During a &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomical_transit" target="_blank"&gt;transit&lt;/a&gt;, Venus can be seen from &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth" target="_blank"&gt;Earth&lt;/a&gt; as a small black dot moving across the face of the Sun. The duration of such transits is usually several hours (the transit of 2012 lasted 6 hours and 40 minutes). A transit &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occultation#Occultations,_transits_and_eclipses" target="_blank"&gt;is similar to&lt;/a&gt; a &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_eclipse" target="_blank"&gt;solar eclipse&lt;/a&gt; by the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon" target="_blank"&gt;Moon&lt;/a&gt;. Although the diameter of Venus is more than three times that of the Moon, Venus appears smaller and travels more slowly across the face of the Sun, because it is much farther away from Earth.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

A member of the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Triumvirate" target="_blank"&gt;First Triumvirate&lt;/a&gt;, Caesar led the Roman armies in the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallic_Wars" target="_blank"&gt;Gallic Wars&lt;/a&gt; before defeating his political rival &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pompey" target="_blank"&gt;Pompey&lt;/a&gt; in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesar%27s_civil_war" target="_blank"&gt;a civil war&lt;/a&gt;, and subsequently became &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_dictator" target="_blank"&gt;dictator&lt;/a&gt; from 49 BC until &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Julius_Caesar" target="_blank"&gt;his assassination&lt;/a&gt; in 44 BC. He played a critical role in &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crisis_of_the_Roman_Republic" target="_blank"&gt;the events that led to the demise of&lt;/a&gt; the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Republic" target="_blank"&gt;Roman Republic&lt;/a&gt; and the rise of the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire" target="_blank"&gt;Roman Empire&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;The &lt;strong&gt;Amish&lt;/strong&gt; (&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English" target="_blank"&gt;/ˈɑːmɪʃ/&lt;/a&gt;; &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_German_language" target="_blank"&gt;Pennsylvania German&lt;/a&gt;: &lt;em&gt;Amisch&lt;/em&gt;; German: &lt;em&gt;Amische&lt;/em&gt;), formally the &lt;strong&gt;Old Order Amish&lt;/strong&gt;, are a group of traditionalist &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anabaptism" target="_blank"&gt;Anabaptist&lt;/a&gt; &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity" target="_blank"&gt;Christian&lt;/a&gt; &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_denomination" target="_blank"&gt;church fellowships&lt;/a&gt; with &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_German_people" target="_blank"&gt;Swiss German&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alsace" target="_blank"&gt;Alsatian&lt;/a&gt; origins.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amish#cite_note-HarryHerr2018-2" target="_blank"&gt;[2]&lt;/a&gt; They are closely related to &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mennonites" target="_blank"&gt;Mennonite&lt;/a&gt; churches, a separate Anabaptist denomination.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amish#cite_note-3" target="_blank"&gt;[3]&lt;/a&gt; The Amish are known for &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simple_living" target="_blank"&gt;simple living&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plain_dress" target="_blank"&gt;plain dress&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_pacifism#Anabaptist_churches" target="_blank"&gt;Christian pacifism&lt;/a&gt;, and slowness to adopt many &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convenience" target="_blank"&gt;conveniences&lt;/a&gt; of modern technology, with a view neither to interrupt family time, nor replace face-to-face conversations whenever possible, and a view to maintain self-sufficiency. The Amish value rural life, manual labor, humility and &lt;em&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordnung#Gelassenheit" target="_blank"&gt;Gelassenheit&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/em&gt; (submission to God's will).&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

Competition-based reality shows typically feature the gradual elimination of participants, either by a panel of judges, by the viewership of the show, or by the contestants themselves.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

      <description>&lt;html&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Joan Crawford&lt;/strong&gt; (born &lt;strong&gt;Lucille Fay LeSueur&lt;/strong&gt;; March 23, 190?&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joan_Crawford#cite_note-birthyear-1" target="_blank"&gt;[Note 1]&lt;/a&gt; – May 10, 1977) was an American actress. She started her career as a dancer in traveling theatrical companies before debuting on &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadway_theatre" target="_blank"&gt;Broadway&lt;/a&gt;. Crawford was signed to a motion picture contract by &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro-Goldwyn-Mayer" target="_blank"&gt;Metro-Goldwyn-Mayer&lt;/a&gt; in 1925. Initially frustrated by the size and quality of her parts, Crawford launched a publicity campaign and built an image as a nationally known &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flapper" target="_blank"&gt;flapper&lt;/a&gt; by the end of the 1920s. By the 1930s, Crawford's fame rivaled MGM colleagues &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norma_Shearer" target="_blank"&gt;Norma Shearer&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greta_Garbo" target="_blank"&gt;Greta Garbo&lt;/a&gt;. Crawford often played hardworking young women who find romance and financial success. These "rags-to-riches" stories were well received by &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression" target="_blank"&gt;Depression&lt;/a&gt;-era audiences and were popular with women. Crawford became one of &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hollywood,_Los_Angeles,_California" target="_blank"&gt;Hollywood&lt;/a&gt;'s most prominent movie stars and one of the highest paid women in the United States, but her films began losing money. By the end of the 1930s, she was labeled "box office poison".&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>

(&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language" target="_blank"&gt;Russian&lt;/a&gt;: Евге́ний Ви́кторович Приго́жин, IPA: &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Russian" target="_blank"&gt;[jɪvˈɡʲenʲɪj ˈvʲiktərəvʲɪtɕ prʲɪˈɡoʐɨn]&lt;/a&gt;; 1 June 1961 – 23 August 2023) was a &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russians" target="_blank"&gt;Russian&lt;/a&gt; mercenary leader and &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_oligarchs" target="_blank"&gt;oligarch&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yevgeny_Prigozhin#cite_note-auto-5" target="_blank"&gt;[4]&lt;/a&gt; He led the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wagner_Group" target="_blank"&gt;Wagner Group&lt;/a&gt; private military company and was a close confidant of Russian president &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Putin" target="_blank"&gt;Vladimir Putin&lt;/a&gt; until launching &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wagner_Group_rebellion" target="_blank"&gt;a rebellion&lt;/a&gt; in June 2023.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yevgeny_Prigozhin#cite_note-6" target="_blank"&gt;[5]&lt;/a&gt; Prigozhin was sometimes referred to as "Putin's chef" because he owned restaurants and catering businesses that provided services to the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kremlin" target="_blank"&gt;Kremlin&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yevgeny_Prigozhin#cite_note-:3-7" target="_blank"&gt;[6]&lt;/a&gt; Once a convict in the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union" target="_blank"&gt;Soviet Union&lt;/a&gt;,&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yevgeny_Prigozhin#cite_note-auto1-8" target="_blank"&gt;[7]&lt;/a&gt; Prigozhin controlled a network of influential companies whose operations, according to a 2020 investigation, were "tightly integrated with &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Defence_(Russia)" target="_blank"&gt;Russia's Defence Ministry&lt;/a&gt; and its intelligence arm, the &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GRU_(Russian_Federation)" target="_blank"&gt;GRU&lt;/a&gt;".&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yevgeny_Prigozhin#cite_note-9" target="_blank"&gt;[8]&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/html&gt;</description>
      <enclosure url="https://www.patreon.com/api/rss/u/2bweW97Ko-8Fw3yE-z_ORgMzF4fMRqXz/e/89168622.mp3?sig=zdLkrfP33YMelpUHLfePOrg2E7ZlNYo28pqLQqEOku0%3D" length="34338410" type="audio/mpeg"/>

      <title>HMS Wager</title>
      <link>https://www.patreon.com/posts/hms-wager-88612902</link>
      <itunes:image href="https://c10.patreonusercontent.com/4/patreon-media/p/post/88612902/91e0bc7320ff4df0b6100b245bf4019f/eyJkIjo3MiwiaCI6MzAwMCwic3RyaXBfYWxwaGEiOjEsInciOjMwMDAsIndlYnAiOjB9/1.png?token-hash=jl6kRT1N9fOOMK2L5BUwPnDKPsTr90izl_P6HgRUjuI%3D"/>
